117.70406pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-19.43877pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z\!\times\!\mathbb{A}_{log}^{r}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 58.04294pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 110.16934pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.43877pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 110.16934pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Applying
\mathcal{F}
and using the Lemma
13.4
we see that the map
\mathcal{F}(U)\to\mathcal{F}(U\setminus Z)
is a quasi-isomorphism. So the map (
13.3
) is also a quasi-isomorphism.
\square

Proof of Theorem
13.1
:

Let
D=D_{1}\cup...\cup D_{r}
be a canonical divisor of
X
and
r
be a number of smooth components. For
r=1
the statement follows from Lemma
13.5
. Let
r>1
. By Lemma
13.5
it enough to prove that the map

\mathcal{F}(X\setminus Z)\to\mathcal{F}(X^{*})

is a quasi-isomorphism. So it suffice to find Zariski covers
\bigsqcup^{n}_{i=1}U_{i}\to X^{*}
and
\bigsqcup^{n}_{i=1}V_{i}\to X\setminus Z
such that for any index
i_{1},...,i_{k}
with
k\leq n
we have

\mathcal{F}(V_{i_{1}}\cap...\cap V_{i_{k}})\simeq\mathcal{F}(U_{i_{1}}\cap...\cap U_{i_{k}}).

Now, let
V_{i}=X\setminus D_{i}
,
U_{i}=X^{*}
and use induction by
r.

\square

Corollary 13.6
.

Let
X
be a log smooth scheme over
k.
Suppose that the underlying scheme
\underline{X}
is smooth. Then the natural map

[X^{*}]\to[X]^{log}

is a weak equivalence.

Proof:
Let us use Yoneda Lemma. Note that by construction for any smooth
Y\in\rm Sm/k
and any log scheme
(Z,\mathcal{M}^{gr}_{Z})
we have

(13.4)

\mathrm{Hom(\mathit{Y(-q)[-p]},\mathbb{D}([Z]^{log}))\simeq}H^{p,q}_{log}(Y\times Z,\mathbb{Q}).

But
H^{p,q}_{log}(Y\times X,\mathbb{Q})\simeq H^{p,q}(Y\times X^{*},\mathbb{Q})
by Theorem
13.1
(let
\mathcal{F}:=H^{p,q}_{log}(Y\times-,\mathbb{Q})
).
\square

Remark 13.7
.

Using Yoneda lemma and Corollary
12.9
one can check that the functor
[-]^{log}
admits descent under log abstract blow-up. That is, for any log abstract blow-up square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

we have the exact triangle

[Z^{\prime}]^{log}\to[Z]^{log}\oplus[X^{\prime}]^{log}\to[X]^{log}.

Motivic tubular neighborhood

Let
\underline{X}
be a smooth scheme,
\underline{D}
be a normal crossing divisor and
\underline{U}=\underline{X}\setminus\underline{D}
. Let us denote by
X
the associated log scheme. Let
D:=(\underline{D},\mathcal{M}_{X}|_{\underline{D}})
. Note that the square

(13.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}}

is a log abstract blow-up square. So by Remark
13.7
and Corollary
13.6
we have the exact triangle

[D]^{log}\longrightarrow[\underline{D}]\oplus[\underline{U}]\longrightarrow[\underline{X}].

This allows us to interpret the motive
\mathrm{M^{log}}(D)
as the
motivic punctured tubular neighborhoods of
\underline{D}
in
\underline{X}
. Let us denote the punctured tubular neighborhoods by
\mathrm{PTN}_{\underline{X}}\underline{D}
. Note that we also have the canonical

exponential map

(13.6)

exp:\mathrm{PTN}_{\underline{X}}\underline{D}\longrightarrow\underline{U}

which induce by the strict closed immersion of log schemes
D\hookrightarrow X.

Remark 13.8
.

Suppose
\mathrm{k}
admits resolution of singularities. Let
\underline{Y}
be a smooth scheme and
\underline{Z}
be an arbitrary closed subscheme. Then by Hironaka principalization theorem (
[
H64
]
, see also Theorem 3.35 of
[
K09
]
) there exists the abstract blow-up

(13.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
\underline{X}
and
\underline{D}
as above and
p^{-1}(\underline{Y}\setminus\underline{Z})\simeq\underline{X}\setminus\underline{D}
.
Then combining
13.5
and
13.7
we get the
log
abstract blow-up

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{Y}}

and, consequently, the exact triangle

[D]^{log}\longrightarrow[\underline{Z}]\oplus[\underline{Y}\setminus\underline{Z}]\longrightarrow[\underline{Y}].

This allows us to interpret
[D]^{log}
as the motivic punctured tubular neighborhoods of
\underline{Z}
in
\underline{Y}
.

14.
Limit motives via log geometry

Unipotent local systems

Let
\mathring{\Delta}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,0<|z|\leq 1\}
be a punctured disk. Let us denote by
DLocSys(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}
the full subcategory of
D(Sh(\mathring{\Delta},\mathbb{Q}))
containing the complexes
K^{\bullet}
such that
\mathcal{H}^{i}(K^{\bullet})
are local systems. Let
DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the full subcategory of
DLocSys(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}
generated by the trivial local system
\underline{\mathbb{Q}}
. By abstract nonsense we have

(14.1)

DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}\simeq\rm A-Mod

where
\rm A=\it REnd(\underline{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq C^{*}(S^{\rm 1},\mathbb{Q})
is the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra of cochains.
Indeed, for any derived local system
M^{\bullet}
we can constructed the
C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})
-module
RHom(\underline{Q},M^{\bullet})
. On the other hand, let
{\bf B}C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})
be the category with one object and the ring of endomorphisms
C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})
. Then the inclusion
{\bf B}C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})
gives rise to the Yoneda extension

{\rm A-Mod\longrightarrow DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}.}

One can check that this functors form the equivalence of categories.

Let
i:pt\hookrightarrow\mathring{\Delta}
be the inclusion of a point. Then we have the inverse image functor

i^{*}:DLocSys(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow D(Sh(pt,\mathbb{Q}))\simeq D(\mathbb{Q}-Vect)

and we can restrict
i^{*}
on
DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}.
On the other hand, the map
i
induces the map of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras

(14.2)

C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow C^{*}(pt,\mathbb{Q})\simeq\mathbb{Q}.

The map
14.2
defines the functor

-\otimes_{C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})}C^{*}(pt,\mathbb{Q}):{\rm A-Mod}\longrightarrow D(\mathbb{Q}-Vect)

Note that
-\otimes_{C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})}C^{*}(pt,\mathbb{Q})
and
i^{*}
commute with colimits. The both maps

REnd_{DLocSys(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\underline{Q})\longrightarrow End_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{Q})

REnd_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{A})\longrightarrow End_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{Q})

coincide with
14.2
. Moreover, this maps differ only by the isomorphism

REnd_{DLocSys(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\underline{Q})\simeq REnd_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathrm{A})

induced by the equivalence
14.1
. So
i^{*}
and
-\otimes_{C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})}C^{*}(pt,\mathbb{Q})
are isomorphic.

Nearby cycles as a bar-construction

Let
C
be a smooth complex curve with marked point 0. Let
f:X\longrightarrow C
be a semi-stable degeneration with a special fiber
X_{s}
. Suppose that
f
is proper and let
\mathring{f}:\mathring{X}\longrightarrow\mathring{C}
be the restriction of
f
on
\mathring{X}:=X\setminus X_{s}.
Then by Ehresmann’s theorem
\mathring{f}
is a fiber bundle. Let
t
be a point of
\mathring{C}
. Using proper base change we conclude that
R^{n}\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}
is a local system with the fiber
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q}).

Now, let us chose a disk
\Delta\subset C
centered at
s
. For
X^{\prime}:=f^{-1}(\Delta)
the map
f:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow\Delta
is also proper semi-stable degeneration. Note the
X^{\prime}
can be considered as a tubular neighborhood of
X_{s}
in
X
. By the previous the complex
R\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}
belongs to
DLocSys(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Lemma 14.1
.

R\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}
is a unipotent derived local system.

Proof:
Note that
R^{m}\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}=H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q})
is zero for sufficiently large
m
. So it enough to check that
R^{b}\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}
belongs to
DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let us denote by
\gamma
the monodromy matrix acts on
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q})
. Note that
\gamma
is a unipotent operator. Now, let
\rm M
be an arbitrary local system with unipotent monodromy
\gamma
. Then
\rm M
belongs to
DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Indeed,
(\gamma-1)^{k}=0
so all eigenvalues are 1. Representing
\rm M
as a direct sum we can assume that
\gamma
is a Jordan block. Then we can use the induction by dimension of
\gamma
.
\square

Note that the equivalence
DLocSys^{uni}(\mathring{\Delta})_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow\rm A-Mod
maps
R\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}
to the complex

RHom_{D(Sh(\mathring{\Delta},\mathbb{Q}))}(\mathbb{Q},R\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q})\simeq RHom_{D(Sh(\mathring{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}))}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q})\simeq C^{*}(\mathring{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q})

such that the structure of
C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})
-module on
R\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q}
induces by the homomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras

C^{*}(\mathring{\Delta},\mathbb{Q})\overset{\mathring{f}^{*}}{\longrightarrow}C^{*}(\mathring{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}).

So for
f:X\longrightarrow C
and
t:pt\hookrightarrow\mathring{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\mathring{C}
we have

(14.3)

t^{*}(R\mathring{f}_{*}\mathbb{Q})\simeq C^{*}(\mathring{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q})\otimes_{C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})}C^{*}(t,\mathbb{Q})

where
\mathring{X}^{\prime}
is a punctured tubular neighborhood of
X_{0}\subset X.

Now, let
\psi_{f}:D(Sh(\mathring{X},\mathbb{Q}))\longrightarrow D(Sh(X_{s},\mathbb{Q}))
be the nearby cycles functor. For semi-stable degeneration
f
we have

H^{*}(X_{s},\psi_{f}\mathbb{Q})\simeq H^{*}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q}).

So, using
14.3
, we get

(14.4)

R\Gamma(X_{s},\psi_{f}\mathbb{Q})\simeq C^{*}(\mathring{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q})\otimes_{C^{*}(S^{1},\mathbb{Q})}C^{*}(t,\mathbb{Q}).

The limit motive of a semi-stable degeneration

Let
k
be an arbitrary field. Again, let
C
be a smooth curve over
k
and
f:X\longrightarrow C
be a semi-stable degeneration. Let us associated with
(X,X_{s})
the canonical log structure. Let
\mathcal{M}^{gr}_{X_{s}}
be the restriction of this structure on
X_{s}.
Note that the map
f
induces the morphism of virtual logarithmic schemes

(14.5)

f:(X_{s},\mathcal{M}^{gr}_{X_{s}})\longrightarrow pt_{log}.

By
7.6
, the map
[X_{s}]^{log}\longrightarrow[pt_{log}]^{log}=[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]
is a homomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-coalgebras. Let
t:\rm Spec(k)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
be a closed point of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
. Then, replicating
14.3
, we define the geometrical motive

{\rm LM}_{f}:=([X_{0}]^{log})^{*}\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}}[Spec(k)].

We will call the motive
{\rm LM}_{f}

the Voevodsky limit motive of f
.

Now, let us fix an inclusion
\tau:k\to\mathbb{C}.
Let
\rm Betti:DM_{gm}(k,\mathbb{Q})\to D(\mathbb{Q}-Vect)
be the associated Betti realization.

Proposition 14.2
.

There exists the canonical quasi-isomorphism

{\rm Betti(LM}_{f})\simeq R\Gamma(X_{s},\psi_{f}\mathbb{Q})

induced by
14.3
.

Proof:
Let
X^{\prime}
and
\mathring{X}^{\prime}
be as above. Note that the inclusion
X_{s}^{an}\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
induces the canonical log analytic structure on
X^{\prime}
. So we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{*}(X_{s}^{log},\mathbb{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{*}(X^{\prime log},\mathbb{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{*}(\mathring{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{*}((pt_{log})^{log},\mathbb{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{*}(\Delta^{log},\mathbb{Q})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{*}(\mathring{\Delta},\mathbb{Q})}}

where each map is a homomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras. Combining Corollary 4.17. of
[
PS08
]
with Theorem
5.2
we conclude that the right-upper and the right-lower horizontal arrows are quasi-isomorphisms.

On the other hand, one can check that the functor
X\mapsto C^{*}(X^{log},\mathbb{Q})
admits descent under virtual logarithmic blow-ups. Indeed, using Theorem
5.2
, we can check this by the same way as in the Section 10. So we get the exact triangle

C^{*}(X^{\prime},\mathbb{Q})\to C^{*}(X^{\prime log},\mathbb{Q})\oplus C^{*}(X_{0}^{an},\mathbb{Q})\to C^{*}(X_{0}^{log},\mathbb{Q}).

But the inclusion
X_{0}^{an}\to X^{\prime}
is a homotopy equivalence. So the left-upper and the left-lower horizontal arrows are quasi-isomorphisms.

It remains to note that, by Proposition
8.6
,
C^{*}((pt_{lpg})^{log},\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow C^{*}(X_{0}^{log},\mathbb{Q})
is the (covariant) Betti realization of the map
[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}\to[X_{0}]^{log*}
.
\square

15.
Motivic monodromy filtration

Limit Hodge structure

Let
\Delta
be a complex unit disk and
f:X\to\Delta
be a proper semi-stable degeneration. By Ehresmann’s theorem the restriction of
f
on
X-X_{0}
is a fiber bundle over
\mathring{\Delta}
. So
f
defines the variation of Hodge structures over
\mathring{\Delta}
Namely, let us fix
t\in\mathring{\Delta}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We will denote by
\check{D}
the
completed local period domain
. Recall that
\check{D}
is the space of flags
...\subset F^{p}\subset...\subset F^{1}\subset F^{0}=H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{C})
with the property
{\rm dim}(F^{p}/F^{p-1})=h^{p,q}(X_{t}).
Note that
f
is proper so
Rf_{*}\mathbb{Q}
defines the local system over
\mathring{\Delta}
with the fibers
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q}).
Then we can define the
local period map

\Phi:\mathring{\Delta}\rightarrow\check{D}

t^{\prime}\in\mathring{\Delta}\mapsto\{{\rm filtration\,on\,\mathit{H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{C})}\,induced\,by\,}{\rm the\,Hodge\,filtration\,on\,}H^{n}(X_{t^{\prime}},\mathbb{C}).\}

Note that an isomorphism
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{C})\simeq H^{n}(X_{t^{\prime}},\mathbb{C})
depends on the choosing of a path so the map
\Phi
is multi-valued. However, one can ask is it possible to continue
\Phi
holomorphically on all
\Delta
?

Let
\gamma
ba a monodromy matrix which act on
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{C})
and
N=-{\rm log}(\gamma)
. Then we can define
\tilde{\Psi}(z):=exp(zN)\Phi(z)
where
z
is the coordinate on the universal cover of
\mathring{\Delta}.
Note that
\tilde{\Psi}(z)=\tilde{\Psi}(z+1)
so we have well-defined map
\Psi:\mathring{\Delta}\to\check{D}
. Then
\Psi
can be holomorphically continue on all
\Delta.

Observe that the extension of
\Psi
defines the new filtration
F_{\rm lim}^{\bullet}
on
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{C})
given by
\Psi(0).
On the other hand, by Borel results, the monodromy matrix
\gamma
is unipotent. So there is some
k
such that
N^{k}=0
and
N
defines one more filtration

...\subset 0\subset W_{0}\subset...\subset W_{2k}=H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q})

with the properties:

-

for each
r
we have
N(W_{r})\subset W_{r-2}
;

-

the operator
N
defines the isomorphisms
{\rm Gr}^{W}_{r}(H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q}))\simeq{\rm Gr}^{W}_{r-2}(H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q})).

Moreover, Schmid proved
[
Sch73
]
that the data

(H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}),W_{\bullet},F^{\bullet}_{\rm lim})

defines the mixed Hodge structure. This Hodge structure called
the limit Hodge structure
.

The Steenbrink construction

There is another, pure algebraic construction of the limit Hodge structure proposed by Steenbrink (see
[
St76
]
,
[
St95
]
and
[
PS08
]
). Namely, he constructed two filtered bicomplexes
(C^{\bullet,\bullet},W(M)^{\bullet,\bullet})_{r})
and
(A^{\bullet,\bullet},W(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\mathbb{C},r})
with
C^{\bullet,\bullet}\in{\rm Bicomp}(Sh(X_{0},\mathbb{Q}))
and
A^{\bullet,\bullet}\in{\rm Bicomp}(Sh(X_{0},\mathbb{C}))
, the filtered quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}\otimes\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathrm{Tot}(A^{\bullet,\bullet})

and the Hodge filtration on
\mathrm{Tot}(A^{\bullet,\bullet})
given by the partial totalizations

F^{p}A={\rm Tot}(s_{\geq p}A^{\bullet,\bullet});\,\,\,s_{\geq r}A^{i,j}=\begin{cases}A^{i,j}&,\,j\geq p\\
0&\rm,\,otherwise.\end{cases}

Steenbrink proved that the data

(15.1)

\psi_{f}\mathbb{Z}^{\rm Hdg}:=(\psi_{f}\mathbb{Z},({\rm Tot}(C^{\bullet,\bullet},{\rm Tot}(W(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}),({\rm Tot}(A^{\bullet,\bullet}),{\rm Tot}(W(M)_{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet,\bullet}),FA))

forms the mixed Hodge complex of sheaves over
X_{0}
. Then the mixed Hodge structure on
\mathbb{H}^{n}(X_{0},\psi_{f}\mathbb{Z}^{\rm Hdg})
is isomorphic to the Schmid limit Hodge structure on
H^{n}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q})
.

Let us describe the bicomplexes
C^{\bullet,\bullet}
and
W(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}
explicitly. Note that we have the canonical log structure on
X
. Let
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}
be the restriction of this structure on
X_{0}.
Then we have the associated exponential complex
exp
given as the cone of the map

\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}^{an}}\overset{exp}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{M}^{gr}_{X_{0}^{an}}.

Moreover,
f
induced the morphism of smooth log analytic spaces
(X^{an},X_{0}^{an})\to(\Delta,0).
The restriction of this morphism on
X_{0}
gives the morphism of virtual log analytic spaces

{(X_{0}^{an},\mathcal{O}^{*}_{an}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{an})\to pt_{log}}
. So we get the element
t\in\Gamma(X_{0}^{an},\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{an}).

Let us define the following bicomplexes:

the bicomplex
W^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}

...\longrightarrow\underset{-1}{0}\longrightarrow\underset{0}{S^{r}(exp)[1]}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}\underset{1}{S^{r+1}(exp)[2]}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}\underset{2}{S^{r+2}(exp)[3]}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}...

where the horizontal differential given by

(15.2)

S^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{an})\otimes\Lambda^{j}(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{an})\overset{{\rm id}\otimes\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}S^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{an})\otimes\Lambda^{j+1}(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}_{an})

and
W_{r}^{p,q}=0
for
p<0;

the bicomplex
\tilde{W}(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}

...\longrightarrow\underset{-1}{0}\longrightarrow\underset{0}{S^{r+1}(exp)[1]}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\underset{1}{S^{r+3}(exp)[2]}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}\underset{2}{S^{r+5}(exp)[3]}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}...

where the differential
d
given by the composition of
15.2
with the inclusion

(15.3)

\mathbb{Q}\otimes S^{i}(exp)\hookrightarrow S^{i+1}(exp)

a\otimes w\mapsto a\cdot w.

Here
\tilde{W}(M)^{p,\bullet}_{r}=S^{r+2p+1}(exp)[p]
and
\tilde{W}(M)^{p,q}_{r}=0
for for
p<0.

Note that we can also define
W(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}
for
r<-1
if we put

S^{k}(-)=0\,\,\,{\rm for}\,\,\,k<0.

Let
\tilde{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}
be the bicomplex

...\longrightarrow\underset{-1}{0}\longrightarrow\underset{0}{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(exp)[1]}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}\underset{1}{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(exp)[2]}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}\underset{2}{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(exp)[3]}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}...\,\,.

Note that we have the canonical inclusions

W^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}\hookrightarrow\tilde{W}(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r+j}

\tilde{W}(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}\hookrightarrow\tilde{W}(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r+j}

W^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}\hookrightarrow\tilde{C}^{\bullet,\bullet}

given by compositions of
15.3
. Let us define

C^{\bullet,\bullet}:=\tilde{C}/W_{0}

W(M)_{r}^{\bullet,\bullet}:={\rm im}(\tilde{W}(M)_{r}^{\bullet,\bullet}\to C^{\bullet,\bullet}).

Now, we should make a few remarks. Let
N
be the number of irreducible components of
X_{0}.
Note that
\tilde{W}(M)^{n}_{r}\subseteq\ W_{0}^{n}
if
r+2n+1\leq n.
So
\tilde{W}(M)^{n}_{r}=0
for
n\leq-r-1.
Let
n>-r
and
r<-(N-1)
. Then
r+2n+1>N
and

({\rm Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}))^{n}=\Lambda^{r+2n+1}\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}[-r-n]=0.

On the other hand, let
n=-r
and
r<-(N-1).
Then
-r+1>N
and
({\rm Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}))^{n}=\Lambda^{-r+1}\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}[-r-n]=0.
Finally, assume that
r>N-1
. Then again
({\rm Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(C^{\bullet,\bullet}))^{n}=0.
So we get

Lemma 15.1
.

The inclusions
W(M)_{r-1}\subset W(M)_{r}
are quasi-isomorphism for
r\leq-(N-1)
and
r\geq N-1.
Moreover, for
r\geq N-1
the canonical inclusions
W(M)^{\bullet,\bullet}_{r}\hookrightarrow C^{\bullet,\bullet}
are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof:
It remains to check that the canonical inclusion

\tilde{W}(M)_{N-1}\hookrightarrow\tilde{C}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Using the spectral sequence of a bicomplex, it can be reduce to the fact that the maps

S^{N-1+2n+1}(exp)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(exp)

are quasi-isomorphisms.
\square

Motivic monodromy filtration

Let
\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1}
be the cokernel of the map

{\rm id}\otimes\psi_{0,N}:\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes 2N-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{N-1}.

We define the increasing filtration

...\,0\subset\mathcal{W}(M)_{-N+1}\subset\mathcal{W}(M)_{-N+2}\subset...\subset\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-2}\subset\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1}

with

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}:={\rm im}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1-r}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{N-2}\cdot...\cdot(({\rm id}\otimes\varphi_{r})}

Remark 15.3
.

Note that
{\rm Gr}^{\mathcal{W}(M)}_{r}(\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1})=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0,-r}\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}^{n}/\mathcal{W}(M)_{r-1}^{n}[-n]
. Indeed, same as in the Steenbrink construction, the n-th differential of
\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}
factors through the map
\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{W}(M)_{r-1}^{n+1}\overset{\varphi_{r-1}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}^{n+1}.
So all differentials of the complex
\rm Gr^{\mathcal{W}(M)}_{r}(\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1})
are zero.
On the other hand, by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
15.1
, we get
(\rm Gr^{\mathcal{W}(M)}_{r}(\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1}))^{n}=0
for
n<-r.

Now, let us define the stable motivic sheaves
[W(M)_{r}]\in\rm DA_{et}(\mathit{X}_{0},\mathbb{Q})
by the formula

[W(M)_{r}]:=\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathcal{W}(M)_{r})(-2(N-1))[-2(N-1)]

and define

[\mathrm{Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(W(M)_{N-1})]:=\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathcal{W}(M)}_{r}(\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1}))(-2(N-1))[-2(N-1)].

Note that we have an exact sequences

[W(M)_{r-1}]\longrightarrow[W(M)_{r}]\longrightarrow[\mathrm{Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(W(M)_{N-1})].

One can think about sheaves
[W(M)_{r}]
as the motivic analog of the filtration
{W(M)_{N-1}\supset W(M)_{N-2}...}
. Now, we will find out some fundamental properties of the motives
[W(M)_{r}]
and
[\mathrm{Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(W(M)_{N-1})].

Lemma 15.4
.

The map
\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes i}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathrm{im}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes i}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\Lambda^{i+j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})
is a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence for any
i
and
j
.

Proof:
It suffice to check that
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}(\mathrm{ker}(f_{i+j-1}\cdot...\cdot f_{j}))=0
. If
i=1
we can consider the Koszul complex

S^{j+1}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\to\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})=...\to S^{k+1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\overset{\partial_{j-k}}{\longrightarrow}S^{k}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k+1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to...

Then we have the exact sequences

\mathrm{im}(\partial_{j-k})\to S^{k}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k+1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\ker(\partial_{j-k-1})

and

\mathrm{im}(\partial_{j-1})\to S^{2}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\ker(f_{j})

But
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}S^{k}\mathcal{O}^{*}=\Lambda^{k}(\mathbb{Q})(k)[k]=0.

If
i>1
note that
f_{i+j+1}...f_{j}
factors through the map

\lambda_{i,j}:\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}

\eta\otimes\omega\mapsto\eta\wedge\omega.

Applying the snake lemma to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(f_{i+j-1}...f_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*i}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{im}(f_{i+j-1}...f_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ker}(\lambda_{i,j})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{im}(\lambda_{i,j})}}

we conclude that the left vertical arrow is a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence.
Now, let us consider two Koszul complexes

S^{i+j}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\overset{\mathrm{id}+i}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})=...\to S^{k+1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})\overset{\partial_{j-k}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}S^{k}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k+1}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})\to...;

S^{i+j}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\overset{\mathrm{id}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{*})=...\to S^{k+1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k}(\mathcal{O}^{*})\overset{\partial_{j-k}^{\prime\prime}}{\longrightarrow}S^{k}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-k+1}(\mathcal{O}^{*}M)\to...;

Then the map
\lambda_{i,j}
can be given as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{i+j}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{i+j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}}

So the kernel of
\lambda_{i,j}
is the intersection of
\mathrm{im}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{i+j-1}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})\overset{\partial_{i+j-1}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{i+j}(\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}))
with
\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}.
Note that the preimage of
\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is the direct sum

(\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{i-1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})\oplus(\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}).

So
\ker(\lambda_{i,j})
is the sum of kernels
\ker(\partial_{i}^{\prime\prime})=\ker(\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{i+1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j-1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})
and
\ker(f_{j})=\ker(\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{i-1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{i-1}\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{j+1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}).

But both of them are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalent to zero by the previous.
\square

Now, let us describe the graded pieces
[\mathrm{Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(W(M)_{N-1})]
explicitly. We will denote by
D_{i}
the irreducible components of
X_{0}=D_{1}\cup...D_{N}.
Let us introduce the notation

D_{J}:=\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D\{m\}:=\bigsqcup_{|J|=m}D_{J}

and denote by
a_{m}
the canonical inclusions
D\{m\}\hookrightarrow X_{0}.

Proposition 15.5
.

For any
r
we have

[\mathrm{Gr}^{W(M)}_{r}(W(M)_{N-1})]\simeq\bigoplus_{n\geq 0,-r}a_{r+2n+1*}\mathbb{Q}_{D\{r+2n+1\}}(-n-r)[-2n-r].

Proof:
Firstly, observe that
\mathcal{\bar{M}}^{gr}=a_{1*}\mathbb{Q}_{D\{1\}}
and
\Lambda^{j}(a_{1*}\mathbb{Q}_{D\{1\}})=a_{j*}\mathbb{Q}_{D\{j\}}.
So by Remark
15.3
it suffice to construct an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence

(15.5)

\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes 2(N-1)-n-r}\otimes\Lambda^{r+2n+1}\bar{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}^{n}/\mathcal{W}(M)_{r-1}^{n}).

Let
\beta
be the canonical map
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)^{n}_{r}\to\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}^{n}.

Let us consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta\cdot\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{r-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1-r+1}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{r-1}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M)_{r-1}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta\cdot\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{r})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1-r}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{r}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi_{r-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Then
15.5
can be defined as the map between cokernel of
{\rm id}\otimes\varphi_{r-1}
and
\mu.
So it suffice to check that
\tilde{\varphi}
is an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence. Let us consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1-j}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)^{n}_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{im}(\beta\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)^{n}_{N-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M)^{n}_{N-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j}}

Suppose we have
\alpha\in\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{N-1}^{n}
and
\beta(\alpha)=0
. Then
\alpha\in\mathrm{im}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes 2N-1}\otimes\Lambda^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to...)
.
Hence
\alpha\in\mathrm{im}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes 2(N-1)-j-n}\otimes\Lambda^{j+2n+1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to...)
and there is some
\tilde{\alpha}
such that
\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j}(\tilde{\alpha})=\alpha
.
Then using commutativity of the diagram we conclude that
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathrm{ker}(\beta\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j})
. So by the snake lemma the left vertical arrow is a surjection. Then for any
j
we get the diagram with exact lines

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}\mathrm{ker}(\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ker}(\beta)}

Note that
\ker(\varphi_{N-2}...\varphi_{j})=\ker(f_{N-1+2n}...f_{j+2n+1})
. So the Proposition follows from Lemma
15.4
.
\square

Relative projection formula.

Suppose
f:X\longrightarrow Y
is a proper morphism of schemes. Let
B\to Rf_{*}C
and
B\to A
be homomorphisms of motivic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras. Then by Remark
4.5
and monoidality of
f^{*}
the maps
f^{*}B\to C
and
f^{*}B\to f^{*}A
are also homomorphisms of motivic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras. So we can forms two relative tensor products
A\otimes_{B}Rf_{*}C
and
f^{*}A\otimes_{f^{*}B}C
.

Proposition 15.6
.

We have the canonical weak equivalence

(15.6)

A\otimes_{B}Rf_{*}C\to Rf_{*}(f^{*}A\otimes_{f^{*}B}C).

Proof:
Note that
f^{*}
is monoidal so
f^{*}Rf_{*}C
has the natural structure of
f^{*}B
-algebra. Moreover, the counit of adjunction
\varepsilon_{C}:f^{*}Rf_{*}C\to C
is a morphism of
f^{*}B
-algebras. Indeed, by Remark
4.5
,
\varepsilon_{C}
is a homomorphism of algebras. The homomorphism
f^{*}B\to C
is adjoint to the homomorphism
B\to Rf_{*}C
. So the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}Rf_{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{C}}

is commutative.
Now, let us define the map
15.6
as the composition

A\otimes_{B}Rf_{*}C\overset{\eta}{\longrightarrow}Rf_{*}f^{*}(A\otimes_{B}Rf_{*}C)\simeq Rf_{*}(f^{*}A\otimes_{f^{*}B}f^{*}Rf_{*}C)\overset{Rf_{*}({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon)}{\longrightarrow}Rf_{*}(f^{*}A\otimes_{f^{*}B}C).

Note that
f
is proper so
Rf_{*}
commutes with geometric realizations. Each maps in the composition can be lifted on the level of the corresponding simplicial sets. So we get the map

(15.7)

(A\otimes_{B}Rf_{*}C)^{\bullet}\longrightarrow Rf_{*}(f^{*}A\otimes_{f^{*}B}C)^{\bullet}

For fixed
n
we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B^{\otimes n}\otimes Rf_{*}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}f^{*}(A\otimes B^{\otimes n}\otimes Rf_{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}(f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B^{\otimes n}\otimes f^{*}Rf_{*}C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}(f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B^{\otimes n}\otimes C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rf_{*}({\rm id}\otimes\varepsilon)}

Note that this maps induces by
the exchange transformation

Ex(f^{*}_{*},\otimes)
(see
[
CD19
]
, Section 1.1.31). So, using projection formula, we conclude that (
15.7
) is a weak equivalence of simplicial objects.
\square

The limit log scheme.

By the relative projection formula we get

\mathrm{LM}_{f}\simeq Rf_{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathrm{Sym^{*}}(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1])}\mathrm{Sym^{*}(M^{gr}(-1)[-1])})

But
\mathrm{Sym^{*}}
is monoidal and commutes with colimits. So

\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathrm{Sym^{*}}(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1])}\mathrm{Sym^{*}(M^{gr}(-1)[-1])}\simeq\mathrm{Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1])}

where
\rm\tilde{M}^{gr}:=\mathbb{Q}\oplus_{\mathbb{Q}(1)[1]\oplus\mathbb{Q}}M^{gr}.
Note that
{\rm\tilde{M}^{gr}}=\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrm{Ex^{log}}(\mathcal{\tilde{M}}^{gr}\otimes\mathbb{Q})
where
\mathcal{\tilde{M}}^{gr}
is the colimit of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{gr}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Moreover, one can note that the sheaf
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}
corresponds to the fiber product

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

in the category of virtual log schemes. Let us denote the fiber product by
X_{\rm lim}
.

Remark 15.7
.

Note that the fiber product make sense only in the context of virtual log geometry. Indeed, there is no morphisms
X_{s}\to X_{s}\times pt_{log}
in the classical category of logarithmic schemes.

By the previous we get

Proposition 15.8
.

The relative projection formula induces the canonical weak equivalence

{\rm LM}_{f}^{*}\simeq[X_{\rm lim}]^{log}.

In particular, any inclusion
k\subset\mathbb{C}
induces the isomorphism

H^{*}(X^{log}_{\rm lim},\mathbb{Q})\simeq H^{*}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q}).

Observe that the sheaf
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}
is closely related to the complexes
\mathcal{W}_{r}
.

Lemma 15.9
.

For any
n
we have

\mathrm{im}(\Lambda^{n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{n+1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})\simeq\mathrm{ker}(\Lambda^{n+1}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\overset{\wedge t}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{n+2}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}})\simeq\Lambda^{n}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}.

As a consequence,

H^{0}(\mathcal{W}_{r})=\Lambda^{r}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr},\,\,\,H^{N-1}(\mathcal{W}_{r})=\Lambda^{r+N-1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}\,\,\,\mathrm{and}\,\,H^{n}(\mathcal{W}_{r})=0\,\,\,\mathrm{for}\,\,\,n\neq 0,N-1.

Remark 15.10
.

Suppose we have two complexes
A,\,B\in Comp^{\bullet}(Sh_{et}(Sm/X_{0},\mathbb{Q}))
together with filtrations
F^{\bullet}A,\,F^{\bullet}B
. Let
g:A\to B
be a filtered morphism. If the induced maps
{\mathrm{Gr}^{FA}_{r}(A)\to\mathrm{Gr}^{FB}_{r}(B)}
are
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalences then so is
g
. In particular,
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}A\simeq 0
if
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\mathrm{Gr}^{FA}(A)\simeq 0
.

Now, let us construct a quasi-isomorphism
{\rm Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1])}\simeq[W(M)_{N-1}].
Firstly, let us consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{N-1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{2N-1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N}\otimes\Lambda^{N-1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}}

The lines of the diagram is exact by Lemma
15.9
. The right vertical arrow is a composition of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalences. So the map
\Lambda^{N-1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathcal{W}_{N})
is a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence.

On the other hand, note that the map

\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{N}\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{N-1}

is a
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalence. Indeed, let we consider the silly filtration on
\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{N}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{N-1}
. Then on the n-th graded pieces we get

\mathrm{id}\otimes(f_{N+2n-1}...f_{N+n}):\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1}\otimes\Lambda^{N+n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes N-1-n}\otimes\Lambda^{N+2n}\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{gr}}.

Again, this map is a composition of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy equivalences and we can use Remark
15.10
. So we proved

Proposition 15.11
.

We have a chain of homotopy equivalences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{*N-1}\otimes\Lambda^{N-1}\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{gr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes 2N-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathcal{W}_{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{O}^{*\otimes 2N-1}\otimes\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{N-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1}}

which induces the quasi-isomorphism

{\rm Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1])}\simeq[W(M)_{N-1}].

Motivic logarithm of monodromy

Firstly, let us recall the classical picture. In
[
PS08
]
, Steenbrink defined the morphism
\nu:\psi^{Hdg}_{f}\mathbb{Q}\to\psi^{Hdg}_{f}\mathbb{Q}(-1)
which maps
W(M)_{r}
to
W(M)_{r-2}
. On the level of the complex
Tot(C^{\bullet,\bullet})
the construction is the following.

Let
K^{\bullet,\bullet}
be a bicomplex. Note that the operation
K^{p,q}\mapsto K^{p+1,q-1}
does not change the totalization. Let us define the map of bicomplexes by the rule

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{r+1}(exp)[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{r+3}(exp)[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{r-2+1}(exp)[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{r-2+3}(exp)[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{r-2+5}(exp)[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

So we get the map

\mathrm{Tot}(\tilde{W}(M)_{r})\to\mathrm{Tot}(\tilde{W}(M)_{r-2}).

We also have the map of bicomplexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{0}(exp)[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}(exp)[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{0}(exp)[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{1}(exp)[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S^{2}(exp)[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}

which induces the map

\mathrm{Tot}(W_{0})\to\mathrm{Tot}(W_{0}).

Then, taken limit by
r
, we get the endomorphism

Tot(C^{\bullet,\bullet})\overset{\nu}{\longrightarrow}Tot(C^{\bullet,\bullet})

such that
\nu(W(M)_{r})\subset W(M)_{r-2}.
Steenbrink proved that
2\pi i\nu
acts on
\mathbb{H}^{k}(X_{0},Tot(C^{\bullet,\bullet}))\simeq H^{k}(X_{t},\mathbb{Q})
as a logarithm of monodromy
\log(\gamma).

It should be noted that
\nu
is nilpotent. Indeed,
\nu^{r+p+1}
maps
S^{r+2p+1}(exp)/S^{p}(exp)\,\,
to
{S^{r+2p+1}(exp)/S^{r+2p+1}(exp)=0.}
But
S^{r+2N+1}(exp)/S^{N}(exp)
is quasi-isomorphic to 0. So
\nu^{r+N+1}=0
in the homotopy category.

Now, let us define a motivic analog of
\nu.
Note that we have the map of complexes

\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{r}\to\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{W}}(M)_{r-2}[1]

which can be defined using the diagram

). So we get the morphism

\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-3}\overset{\nu}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{W}(M)_{N-1}[1]

which maps
\mathcal{O}^{*}\otimes\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}
to
\mathcal{W}(M)_{r-2}[1].
It suffice to apply
\Sigma^{\infty}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}
,
L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
and
-\otimes\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1]
and get the maps

[W(M)_{r}]\overset{\nu}{\longrightarrow}[W(M)_{r-2}](-1).

Moreover, using Proposition
15.11
we can define the
motivic logarithm of monodromy

\mathrm{Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1]))}\overset{\nu}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1]))}(-1)

such that all the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1]))}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym^{*}(\tilde{M}^{gr}(-1)[-1]))}(-1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{W}(M)_{r}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{W}(M)_{r-2}](-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

is commutative. Notice that
\nu
is nilpotent by the same arguments as in the classical case.

16.
Relations with Ayoub’s nearby cycles

Specialization systems

Let
B
be a scheme together with closed and open subsets
Z,\,U\subset B
. Following Ayoub
[
Ayo07
]
, a
specialization system
is a collection of functors

Sp_{f}:DA_{et}(X_{U},\mathbb{Q})\to DA_{et}(X_{Z},\mathbb{Q}),

one for each
B
-scheme
f:X\to B
, satisfied a certain list of axioms. In particular, for any composition
Y\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}X\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B
we have the natural transformations

\alpha_{g}:g^{*}Sp_{f}\to Sp_{fg}g^{*},\,\,\,\,\,\,\beta_{g}:Sp_{f}Rg_{*}\to Rg_{*}Sp_{fg}

such that
\alpha_{g}
is an equivalence when
g
is smooth and
\beta_{g}
is an equivalence when
g
is proper.

Let
\varphi:B^{\prime}\to B
be a morphism of scheme. Let
Z^{\prime}:=\varphi^{-1}(Z)
and
U^{\prime}:=\varphi^{-1}(U).
Using
f
, one can restrict a specialization system from
(Z,B,U)
to
(Z^{\prime},B^{\prime},U^{\prime})
by an obvious way.

Now, let us consider the following category:

•

the objects given by pairs
(C,D)
where
C
is curve over k and
D
is a divisor;

•

the morphisms between
(C,D)
and
(C^{\prime},D^{\prime})
given by morphisms
f:C\to C^{\prime}
such that
f^{-1}(D^{\prime})=D
.

Lemma 16.1
.

Suppose we choose for each
(D,C,C\setminus D)
a specialization system
Sp^{(C,D)}
such that the collection
\{Sp^{(C,D)}\}
is coherent under restrictions. Then the collection can be recovered using
Sp^{(\mathbb{A}^{1},0)}
.

Proof:
Firstly, note that the collection uniquely defined by
Sp^{(C,D)}
with affine
C.
Indeed, let
W_{j}\hookrightarrow C
be an open affine cover of an arbitrary
C
and
f:X\to C
be a
C
-scheme. Let us denote by
\nu_{j}
the inclusions
X_{W_{j}}\hookrightarrow X
. Then

\nu_{j}^{*}Sp^{(C,D)}_{f}\simeq Sp^{(C,D)}_{f\nu_{j}}\nu_{j}^{*}=Sp^{(W_{j},D_{j})}_{f\nu_{j}}\nu_{j}^{*}.

So we can recover
Sp^{(C,D)}
by gluing together
Sp^{(W_{j},D_{j})}_{f\nu_{j}}\nu_{j}^{*}.

On the other hand, for affine
C
we can choose
g:C\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
such that
D=g^{-1}(0).
Then
Sp^{(C,D)}
is the restriction of
Sp^{(\mathbb{A}^{1},0)}
.
\square

Motivic nearby cycles

Let
(C,s)
be a curve with a marked point. In
[
Ayo07
]
, Ayoub constructed
unipotent motivic nearby cycles

\Upsilon_{*}
as a specialization system over
(s,C,\mathring{C}).
By the previous it suffice to define
\Upsilon_{*}
for
C=\mathbb{A}^{1},\,\,\,s=0.

Let
f:X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
be an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-scheme. Let us consider the diagram

This is a diagram of coalgebras in
Sm/\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}.
Note that the canonical functor
{Sm/\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\to DA_{et}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q})}
is monoidal. So we get the diagram of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-coalgebras in
DA_{et}(\mathring{X},\mathbb{Q})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}(1)[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

and the cosimplicial object
\rm coBar_{\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}(1)[1]}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}})^{\bullet}.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a motivic sheaf over
\mathring{X}
. Then, following Ayoub,

\Upsilon_{f}\mathcal{F}:=|i^{*}Rj_{*}(\underline{Hom}(\rm coBar_{\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}(1)[1]}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}})^{\bullet},\mathcal{F}))|.

Remark 16.2
.

Let
U
be a Zariski neighborhood of
s.
Let us consider the composition
X_{U}\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}X\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}C.
Then
\Upsilon_{f}(\mathcal{F})=g^{*}\Upsilon_{f}(\mathcal{F})\simeq\Upsilon_{fg}(g^{*}\mathcal{F}).
So we get well defined functor

\Upsilon_{f}:DA_{et}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Q})\to DA_{et}(X_{\eta},\mathbb{Q}).

The equivalence of construction

Let
f:X\to C
be a proper semi-stable degeneration. Now, we want to construct a natural weak equivalence between the limit motive
\mathrm{LM}_{f}
and
\Upsilon_{\mathrm{id}}([X_{\eta}]^{*}).
Let us start from the following

Lemma 16.3
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth scheme with smooth
X
. Let
j:X-D\hookrightarrow X
be the open immersion. Then there is a natural weak equivalence

\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{M^{gr}_{(X,D)}}(-1)[-1])\simeq Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}.

Proof:
We have the natural isomorphism of algebras
j^{*}\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{M^{gr}_{(X,D)}}(-1)[-1])\simeq\mathbb{Q}.
So we get the homomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{M^{gr}_{(X,D)}}(-1)[-1])\longrightarrow Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}.
Let
Y
be a scheme smooth over
X.
Then, by Corollary
13.6
and the construction of functoriality of
H^{p,q}_{log}
, the map

\mathrm{Hom}(Y(q)[p],\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{M^{gr}_{(X,D)}}(-1)[-1]))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(Y(q)[p],Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q})

is a quasi-isomorphism for any
p
and
q\,\,\,\,\square.

By the previous, the algebra
\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{\tilde{M}^{gr}}(-1)[-1])
is equivalent to the bar construction associated with the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{M^{gr}_{X_{0}}}(-1)[-1])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}_{X_{0}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{X_{0}}(-1)[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{X_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

Note that
f
comes from the global section of
\mathcal{M}^{gr}_{(X,D)}.
So the diagram can be extended to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}_{X}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{X}(-1)[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

So we get

\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{\tilde{M}^{gr}}(-1)[-1])\simeq i^{*}(Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{X}^{*}}\mathbb{Q}_{X})\simeq|i^{*}(Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{X}^{*}}\mathbb{Q}_{X}^{\bullet})|.

Moreover,

i^{!}((Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}})\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{X}^{*}}\mathbb{Q}_{X}^{n})=i^{!}(Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\otimes(\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1])^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathbb{Q})\simeq\oplus i^{!}Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}(-k)[-k]=0.

So

(Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}})\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{X}^{*}}\mathbb{Q}_{X}^{\bullet}\simeq Rj_{*}j^{*}((Rj_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}})\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{X}^{*}}\mathbb{Q}_{X}^{\bullet})\simeq Rj_{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathring{X}}^{*}}\mathbb{Q})^{\bullet}

and

(16.1)

\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{\tilde{M}^{gr}}(-1)[-1])\simeq|i^{*}Rj_{*}(\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathring{X}}^{*}}\mathbb{Q})^{\bullet}|

Here the relative tensor product is the product associated with the diagram

(16.2)

Theorem 16.4
.

Suppose that
k
satisfies the Beilinson-Soulé vanishing conjecture. Then

{\mathrm{Sym}^{*}(\mathrm{\tilde{M}^{gr}}(-1)[-1])\simeq\Upsilon_{f}(\mathbb{Q})}

and, consequently,

\mathrm{LM}_{f}\simeq\Upsilon_{\mathrm{id}}([X_{\eta}]^{*}).

Proof:
It suffice construct the first equivalence. By Remark
16.2
we may assume that
C
is affine. Let us choose a function
g
on
C
with the property
g^{-1}(0)=s
and denote by
\varphi
the composition
X\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{A}^{1}.
Note that
\Upsilon_{f}\mathbb{Q}
can be given by the formula
|i^{*}Rj_{*}(\rm Bar_{\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}(-1)[-1]}(\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}},\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}})^{\bullet})|
where the bar construction associated with the diagram

(16.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}(-1)[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{\mathring{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{*}}

Then, according
16.1
, it enough to check that the diagrams
16.2
and
16.3
define equivalent bar constructions.

Note that the map
\Delta^{*}
is dual to the global section
\varphi=g\cdot f
of sheaf
\mathcal{O}^{*}
on
\mathring{X}.
On the other hand, the map
\mu
corresponding to the map
(id,\varphi):\mathcal{O}^{*}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\to\mathcal{O}^{*}.
The both map can be represented as the pullbacks from
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
under
\varphi.
So we should compare the diagrams

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.06042pt}{-13.96371pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras such that
g^{\prime}\alpha=g
,
B^{\prime}=B\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}=f\otimes_{A}A^{\prime}.
Then
B^{\prime}\otimes_{A}^{\prime}C\simeq B\otimes_{A}C
as objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}.
Indeed, by results of (
[
HA
]
, Section 4.4.3),

B^{\prime}\otimes_{A}^{\prime}C=(B\otimes_{A}A^{\prime})\otimes_{A}^{\prime}C\simeq B\otimes_{A}(A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}^{\prime}C)\simeq B\otimes_{A}C.

Remark 16.6
.

Let
B
and
B^{\prime}
be as above. Then
B\simeq B^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\otimes}.
In fact,
\alpha:A\to A^{\prime}
induce the weak equivalence
B\simeq A\otimes_{A}B\to A^{\prime}\otimes_{A}B.

Remark 16.7
.

Note that the unit of adjunction
\mathrm{Sym}^{*}([\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*})\overset{\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}
is a weak equivalence of algebras. Indeed, by conservativity of the forgetful functor it suffice to check that
\epsilon
is a weak equivalence of the motivic sheaves. But the natural inclusion
[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{*}([\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*})
is an equivalence and we can use the triangle identities.

Let us consider the automorphism

(1,-1):\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1].

By the Remark
16.7
it is an automorphism of algebras. For any module
M
let us denote by
M\otimes_{(1,-1)}[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}
the tensor product
M\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1]}\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1]
with respect to
(1,-1)
. Then
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{(1,-1)}[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}\simeq\mathbb{Q}
and
(1,t)\otimes_{(1,-1)}[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}=(1,-t)
in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras. Indeed, the first equivalence follows from Remark
16.6
. To prove the second equality, let us consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}\simeq\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{(1,-1)}[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,t)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(b,c)}

Note that
\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{(1,-1)}[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]^{*}
is an algebra with unit. So
a=b=1.
Then, by commutativity,
c=-t.

Now, one can apply Remark
16.5
to prove the Theorem
16.4
. Namely, we should check that the homomorphisms
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}(-1)[-1]\overset{(1,t)^{*}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}
and
{\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}\oplus\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}(-1)[-1]\overset{(1,-t)}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}}
are coincide in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras.
Since the algebra
[\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}]_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}^{*}
is free it suffice to check that
(1,t)^{*}=(1,-t)
in the homotopy category of motivic sheaves
DA_{et}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q})_{\setminus\mathbb{Q}}.
Then the Theorem follows from the Lemma.

Lemma 16.8
.

Suppose that
k
satisfies the Beilinson-Soulé vanishing conjecture. Let
{f:\mathbb{Q}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(1)[1]}
be a morphism in
DA_{et}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q}).
Let
\check{f}
and
f(-1)[-1]
be the images of
f
in
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1],\mathbb{Q})
under the functors
\mathrm{Hom}(-,\mathbb{Q})
and
-\otimes\mathbb{Q}(-1)[-1].
Then
\check{f}=-f(-1)[-1].

Proof:
Let
DTM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset DA_{et}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q})
be the triangulated subcategory of Tate motives. By result of
[
Lev93
]
, there is weight t-structure on
DTM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
together with tensor exact functors

gr^{W}_{q}:DTM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow T_{q}

where
T_{q}\simeq D(Vect_{\mathbb{Q}})
is a full triangulated subcategory of
DTM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset DA_{et}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q})
generated by
\mathbb{Q}(q)[n]
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}.

By assumption,
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
is also satisfies Beilinson-Soulé conjecture. Indeed,

H^{p}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q}(q))=H^{p-1}(k,\mathbb{Q}(q-1))\oplus H^{p}(k,\mathbb{Q}(q)).

So we can apply Theorem 1.4 of
[
Lev93
]
and get the following:

•

There is the non-degenerate t-structure on
DTM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
with the heart
TM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
which consists
M\in DTM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that all
gr^{W}_{i}(A)\simeq\mathbb{Q}(-i)^{n_{i}}
for some
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N};

•

The category
TM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
is closed under extensions and

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{TM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}})}(B,A)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{DA_{et}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},\mathbb{Q})}(B[-1],A);

•

The fiber functor
\rm\oplus_{i}gr^{W}_{i}:TM(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}})_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow Vect_{\mathbb{Q}}
is a faithful exact tensor functor. By the same arguments as
[
DG05
]
, the group
\rm Aut(\oplus_{i}gr^{W}_{i})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\ltimes U
where
U
is a pro-unipotent algebraic group.

Now, let
\mathbb{Q}(1)\to A\to\mathbb{Q}
be the extension associated with
f
and let

\mathbb{Q}\to\check{A}\to\mathbb{Q}(-1);\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathbb{Q}\to A(-1)\to\mathbb{Q}(-1)

be the extensions associated with
\check{f}
and
f(-1)[-1].
It suffice to construct an appropriate map of the extensions in the category of representations of
G=\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\ltimes U
. Let
(\lambda,u)
be an element of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\ltimes U
. Let
(v_{1},v_{2})
be the basis of
A
such that
v_{1}
corresponds to the inclusion
\mathbb{Q}(1)\to A.
By construction, the action of
G
preserves the weight filtration so

\rho(\lambda,u)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda&a_{(\lambda,u)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Then the action of
(\lambda,u)
on
A(-1)
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}1&a_{(\lambda,u)}\lambda^{-1}\\
0&\lambda^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Finally, the action on
\check{A}
given by

(\rho(\lambda,u)^{-1})^{\mathrm{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{-1}&0\\
-a_{(\lambda,u)}\lambda^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}

where
\delta_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}
corresponds to the inclusion
\mathbb{Q}\to\check{A}.
So we have the map of extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

where
B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
with respect to the basis
v_{1},v_{2}
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\,\,\,\square.

Appendix A
The inverse image of an algebraic group

Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is an abelian algebraic group over
\mathrm{k}
.
Then the for each
X\in\mathrm{Sch/k}
the group scheme
\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathrm{k}}X

defines the sheaf
\mathcal{G}_{X}\in\mathrm{Sh_{et}(}\mathrm{Sm}/X,\mathbb{Z})
.
Suppose that there is a morphism
f\!:\!Y\longrightarrow X
. For
any scheme
U
smooth over
X
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{G}(U)
the
composition

(U\overset{\varphi}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G})\longmapsto(U\times_{X}Y\longrightarrow U\overset{\varphi}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G})

define the map

f_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathcal{G}_{X}\longrightarrow f_{*}\mathcal{G}_{Y}

where
f_{*}:\mathrm{Sh_{et}(}\mathrm{Sm}/Y,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Sh_{et}(}\mathrm{Sm}/X,\mathbb{Z})
.
Let
f_{\mathcal{G}}^{\natural}
be the adjoint map.

Theorem A.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is smooth over
k
. Then for any morphism

f\!:\!Y\longrightarrow X
the map
f_{\mathcal{G}}^{\natural}:f^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{Y}

is an isomorphism.

Proof:
Let
G
be an abelian group. Let
I\subset\mathbb{Z}[G]

be the subgroup generated by
[0]
and
J\subset\mathbb{Z}[G\times G]

be the subgroup generated by all
[(0,g)]
and
[(g,0)]
for any

g\in G
. Let us define the complex
Q_{\geq-1}^{*}(G)
:

\underset{-1}{\mathbb{Z}[G\times G]/J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underset{0}{\mathbb{Z}[G]/I}

where
d([(g_{1},g_{2})])=[g_{1}]+[g_{2}]-[g_{1}+g_{2}]
.

Now, let
S
be a scheme. Then we can define the complex of presheaves

\boldsymbol{Q}_{\geq-1}(\mathcal{G}_{X})
on
\mathrm{Sm/}S
by
the rule

U\longmapsto Q_{\geq-1}^{*}(\mathcal{G}(U)).

Note that
H^{0}(Q_{\geq-1}^{*}(G))=G
for any
G
. So
H^{0}(\boldsymbol{Q}_{\geq-1}(\mathcal{G}_{X}))=\mathcal{G}_{X}
.
For any
f:Y\longrightarrow X
we have the canonical map

(A.1)

\boldsymbol{Q}_{\geq-1}(\mathcal{G}_{X})\longrightarrow f_{*}\boldsymbol{Q}_{\geq-1}(\mathcal{G}_{Y})

induced by
\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}}]
and
\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}}]
.
The map
f_{\mathcal{G}}
is obtained from
A.1

by applying of the functor
H^{0}
. So it suffice to check that the
map adjoint to
A.1
is an isomorphism.

Observe that
f^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}]=\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}]

by definition of
f^{-1}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}}]

and
\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}}]
are the units
of adjunction. The map

(A.2)

\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}]/I_{X}\longrightarrow f_{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}]/I_{Y})

is obtained by applying of the functor
H^{0}
to the morphism of
complexes

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[pt_{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}[f_{pt}]}
\scriptstyle{[0]}
\textstyle{f_{*}\mathbb{Z}[pt_{Y}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[0]\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,.}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}}]}
\textstyle{f_{*}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}]}

The horizontal map are the units of adjunction. So the adjoint morphism
is an identity and
A.2
is an isomorphism. Analogically,
the map

(A.3)

\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}\times\mathcal{G}_{X}]/J_{X}\longrightarrow f_{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}\times\mathcal{G}_{Y}]/J_{Y})

is obtained from the morphism of complexes

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}}]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}}]}
\scriptstyle{i_{(0,1)}+i_{(1,0)}}
\textstyle{f_{*}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,.}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{X}\times\mathcal{G}_{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}[f_{\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}}]}
\textstyle{f_{*}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{G}_{Y}\times\mathcal{G}_{Y}]}

So the map adjoint to
A.3
is an isomorphism by
the same arguments.
\square

Corollary A.2
.

For a scheme
S
let us denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sm/}S}^{*}

the étale sheaf of invertible functions on
\mathrm{Sm/}S
. Let
f\!:\!Y\longrightarrow X

be a morphism and
f^{\natural}:f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sm/}X}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sm/}Y}^{*}

be the canonical map. Then the map
f^{\natural}
is an isomorphism.

Appendix B
(
\infty
,1)-Grothendieck construction

In classical category theory the Grothendieck construction provide
the equivalence of categories between the category of Grothendieck
fibration over the category
C
and the category of pseudofunctors

\mathrm{Fun}(C^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Cat})
. The similar pictures
exists in the word of infinity categories. The notation of Cartesian
fibrations of simplicial sets is a direct analog of Grothendieck fibrations
of 1-categories. Moreover, for any simplicial set
S
we
have the canonical equivalence of
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{Fun}_{\infty}(S^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})\simeq\mathbf{Cart}_{S}

where
\mathbf{Cart}_{S}
is the category of Cartesian fibrations
over
S
.
This equivalence can be describe using the straightening and unstraightening
functors:

\mathrm{Un^{+}}\!:\!\mathrm{Fun}_{\infty}(S^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Cat}_{(\infty,1)})\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Cart}_{S}\!:\!\mathrm{St^{+}}

The interested reader can find all details in Chapter 3.2 of
[
HTT
]
. We will
abuse the notation and denote by
\int_{C}F
the value of functor

\mathrm{Un^{+}}
on the presheaf
F
. In fact, we will only consider the case when
S
is the nerve of a 1-category
C
.

Relative nerve construction.

Let
F:C\longrightarrow SSet
be a 1-functor. We define the simplicial
set
N_{F}(C)
as follows. Let
J
be a linear order set with
J=|n|
. One can consider
J
as a category. We will denote by
\Delta^{J}
the associated simplicial set. The n-cell of
N_{F}(C)
contained the following data:

(RN1)

A functor
\sigma
from
J
to
C
.

(RN2)

For every nonempty subset
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
having a maximal
element
j_{max}^{\prime}
, a map
{\tau(J^{\prime}):\,\triangle^{J^{\prime}}\rightarrow F(\sigma(j_{max}^{\prime}))}

together with the condition

(RN3)

For nonempty subsets
J^{\prime\prime}\subseteq J^{\prime}\subseteq J
, with
maximal elements
j_{max}^{\prime\prime}\in J^{\prime\prime}
,
j_{max}^{\prime}\in J^{\prime},
the diagram

\textstyle{\triangle^{J^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(\sigma(j_{max}^{\prime\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\sigma(j_{max}^{\prime\prime})\longrightarrow(\sigma(j_{max}))}
\textstyle{\triangle^{J^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(\sigma(j_{max}^{\prime}))}

is required to commute.

The simplicial set
N_{F}(C)
is called
the relative nerve of

F
. Suppose
F
takes value in weak Kan complexes. Then
N_{F}(C)
can be identified with
\int_{C}F
(
[
HTT
]
, Section 3.2.5).

Remark B.1
.

Note that a 0-simplex of
N_{F}(C)
is a pair
(c,Y)
where
c\in Ob(C)
and
Y
is a 0-simplex of
F(c)
. Moreover, a 1-simplex of
N_{F}(C)
is also a pair which contains a morphism
\alpha:c_{1}\to c_{2}
of
C
and a 1-simplex
F(\alpha)(Y_{1})\to Y_{2}
. So the relative nerve compatible with the classical Grothendieck construction.

Example B.2
.

Let
S
be a simplicial set and
\underline{S}:C\longrightarrow SSet

be the constant functor. Then
N_{\underline{S}}(C)
is the Cartesian
product
S\times N(C)
. Indeed, the maps from the condition (RN2)
is just a point of
S_{|J^{\prime}|}
. By (RN3) all such maps unique defined
by the one map
\tau(J)
.

Presentable fibrations.

Following
[
HTT
]
we will call a map of simplicial
sets
{p:X\to S}
a
presentable fibration

if it is a Cartesian and cocartesian fibration such that
each fiber
{X_{s}=X\times_{S}\{s\}}
is a presentable
\infty
-category.

Proposition B.3
.

(Proposition 5.5.3.3. of
[
HTT
]
)

•

Let
p\!:\!X\rightarrow S
be a Cartesian fibration of simplicial
sets, classified by a map
{\chi:S^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}}

Then
p
is a presentable fibration if and only if
\chi
factors
through
\mathbf{Pr}^{\mathrm{R}}\subseteq\mathbf{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}
.

•

Let
p\!:\!X\rightarrow S
be a cocartesian fibration of simplicial
sets, classified by a map
{\chi:S\longrightarrow\mathbf{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}}

Then
p
is a presentable fibration if and only if
\chi
factors
through
\mathbf{Pr}^{\mathrm{L}}\subseteq\mathbf{Cat}_{(\infty,1)}

Corollary B.4
.

(Corollary 5.5.3.4. of
[
HTT
]
,) For every simplicial set S, there
is a canonical bijection
[S,\mathbf{Pr}^{\mathrm{L}}]\simeq[S^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Pr}^{\mathrm{R}}]

where
[S,C]
denotes the collection of equivalence classes of objects
of
\mathbf{Fun}_{\infty}(S,C)
.

For
F:C\longrightarrow\mathbf{Pr}^{\mathrm{R}}
let us denote by

F^{\rm adj}
the corresponding functor
C^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Pr}^{\mathrm{L}}.
Note that
F^{\rm adj}
can be described as follows:

•

F^{\rm adj}(c)=F(c)
for any
c\in C
;

•

F^{\rm adj}(\alpha)
is right-adjoint to
F(\alpha)
for any
\alpha:c_{1}\to c_{2}
. Further, we will denote
F^{\rm adj}(\alpha)
and
F(\alpha)
by
\alpha^{*}
and
\alpha_{*}
.

According to the Corollary,
\int_{C}(F^{\mathrm{op}})

and
(\int_{C^{\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}
are
equivalent as
\infty
-categories.

Remark B.5
.

Let
S=\Delta^{1}
. Then the data
F:\Delta^{1}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{\rm R}
is equivalent to the pair of categories
F(0)
and
F(1)
together with a pair of adjoint functors
\alpha^{*}
and
\alpha_{*}:

\alpha^{*}:F(1)\to F(0):\alpha_{*}.

Then the corresponding relative nerves can be described as follows:

•

the objects of
\int_{\Delta^{1}}(F^{\mathrm{op}})
are the disjoint union of objects
F(0)
and
F(1)
. For any
x,y\in Ob(\int_{C}(F^{\mathrm{op}}))
we have

\mathrm{Hom}(x,y)^{\bullet}=\begin{cases}\varnothing&\mathrm{if}\,\,x\in F(1),y\in F(0)\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{F(i)}(y,x)^{\bullet}&\mathrm{if}\,\,x,y\in F(i)\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{F(1)}(y,\alpha_{*}(x))^{\bullet}&\mathrm{if}\,\,x\in F(0),y\in F(1)\end{cases}.

•

the objects of
(\int_{{\Delta^{1}}^{\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}
are the disjoint union of objects
F(0),\,F(1)
and

\mathrm{Hom}(x,y)^{\bullet}=\begin{cases}\varnothing&\mathrm{if}\,\,x\in F(1),y\in F(0)\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{F(i)}(y,x)^{\bullet}&\mathrm{if}\,\,x,y\in F(i)\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{F(0)}(\alpha^{*}(y),x)^{\bullet}&\mathrm{if}\,\,x\in F(0),y\in F(1)\end{cases}.

Note that for any
x\in F(0)
and
y\in F(1)
we have the canonical map

(B.1)

\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{F(1)}(y,\alpha_{*}(x))\to\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{F(0)}(\alpha^{*}(y),x)

which is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets. Moreover, the maps
B.1
define the functor

\!\int_{\Delta^{1}}(F^{\mathrm{op}})\longrightarrow(\int_{\Delta^{1\,\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}

which is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.

Construction of the equivalence.

Let
C
be a 1-category and
F:N(C)\to\mathbf{Pr}^{\rm R}
be a functor.
Let us construct the canonical equivalence of categories
\int_{C}(F^{\mathrm{op}})
and
(\int_{C^{\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}.

First of all, observe that by (RN3) the functors
\tau(J^{\prime})
factor
through the simplicial subsets

\mathrm{Hom_{\mathit{F^{op}(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{max}))}}^{\bullet}\!(}\alpha_{*}(\tau(j_{min}^{{}^{\prime}})),\tau(j_{max}^{{}^{\prime}}))\subset F(\sigma(j_{max}^{\prime})).

Here
\alpha_{*}\!\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\!F^{op}(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{min}\longrightarrow j^{\prime}_{max})

and

\tau(j_{min}^{{}^{\prime}})\!:\!\triangle^{0}\longrightarrow F^{op}(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{min}))

\tau(j_{max}^{{}^{\prime}})\!:\!\triangle^{0}\longrightarrow F^{op}(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{max}))

are the morphisms corresponded to the inclusions
j_{min}^{{}^{\prime}}\subset J^{\prime}\subset J
,

j_{max}^{{}^{\prime}}\subset J^{\prime}\subset J
.

With this in mind we can define the morphisms

\varphi_{n}\!:\!N_{F^{op}}(C)_{n}\longrightarrow N_{F^{\rm adj}}(C^{op})_{n}.

For
n=0
this is identity map. For
n>0
we map the data (RN1)
and (RN2) to the following data:

\rm(RN1^{adj})

the same functor
\sigma
from
J^{op}
to
C^{op}
.

\rm(RN2^{adj})

For every nonempty subset
J^{\prime op}\subseteq J^{op}
having
the
maximal
element
j_{min}^{\prime}
, the map
\tau(J^{\prime op})^{\vee}:\triangle^{J^{\prime}}\longrightarrow F(\sigma(j_{min}^{\prime}))

which define as the composition

\textstyle{\triangle^{J^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\tau(J^{\prime op})^{\vee}}
\textstyle{F(\sigma(j_{min}^{\prime}))}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom_{\mathit{F(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{max}))}}^{\bullet}\!(}\tau(j_{max}^{{}^{\prime}}),\alpha_{*}\tau(j_{min}^{{}^{\prime}})\!)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom_{\mathit{F(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{min}))}}^{\bullet}}(\alpha^{*}\tau(j_{max}^{{}^{\prime}}),\tau(j_{min}^{{}^{\prime}})\!)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Here
\alpha^{*}\!\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\!F^{\rm adj}(\sigma(j^{\prime}_{max}\longrightarrow j^{\prime}_{min}))

(recall that we replace
J
with
J^{op}
). Notice that the condition
(RN3) for data (
\rm RN1^{adj}
) and (
\rm RN2^{adj}
) automatically satisfied.

Proposition B.6
.

The map
\varphi
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories.

Proof:
It suffice to check that for any pair
(c_{1},Y_{1})
and
(c_{2},Y_{2})
the map

\varphi:\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{\int_{C}(F^{\mathrm{op}})}((c_{1},Y_{1}),(c_{2},Y_{2}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{(\int_{C^{\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}}((c_{1},Y_{1}),(c_{2},Y_{2}))

is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets. Note that we have the the commutative diagram of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{\int_{C}(F^{\mathrm{op}})}((c_{1},Y_{1}),(c_{2},Y_{2}))|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{(\int_{C^{\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}}((c_{1},Y_{1}),(c_{2},Y_{2}))|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Hom}_{C}(c_{1},c_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

and
\varphi
maps
\pi_{1}^{-1}(\alpha)
to
\pi_{2}^{-1}(\alpha)
for any
\alpha
. Moreover,
\pi_{i}^{-1}(\alpha)
and
\pi_{i}^{-1}(\alpha^{\prime})
lie in different connection components for different
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}.
Hence it suffice to prove that
\varphi
induces the weak equivalence

\Delta^{1}\times_{C}\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{\int_{C}(F^{\mathrm{op}})}((c_{1},Y_{1}),(c_{2},Y_{2}))\to\Delta^{1}\times_{C}\mathrm{Hom}^{\bullet}_{(\int_{C^{\mathrm{op}}}F^{\rm adj})^{\mathrm{op}}}((c_{1},Y_{1}),(c_{2},Y_{2}))

for any
\alpha:\Delta^{1}\to C.

On the other hand, for any 1-functor
C^{\prime}\to C
the fiber product
C^{\prime}\times_{C}\int_{C}F

compatible with the inverse image
C^{\prime}\to C\overset{F}{\to}\mathbf{Pr}^{\rm R}.
So we may assume that
C=\Delta^{1}
. In this case the statement follows from Remark
B.5
.
\square
